is reversible and invariant under space translations, respectively.

Remark 3.2
.

The system (
3.5
) has also an other symmetry. Defining the involution

\mathscr{I}(r_{+},r_{-})\triangleq-(r_{-},r_{+}),

then it is clear from (
3.3
), (
3.7
) and (
3.10
) that

\mathcal{E}\circ\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{E}.

However, this last symmetry will not be used along this study.

3.2
Linear dynamics

The purpose of this subsection is to give the expression of the linearized operator at a general state and then to study the associated solutions for the linearized system at the equilibrium state
(r_{+},r_{-})=(0,0).

Linearizing (
1.11
) at a generic state
r=(r_{+},r_{-})
in the direction
(\rho_{+},\rho_{-})
yields

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon r}(a)\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon r}(a)\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}v_{+}-\tfrac{1}{2a}\partial_{xx}^{-1}&\tfrac{1}{2a}\partial_{xx}^{-1}\vskip 2.84544pt\\
\tfrac{1}{2a}\partial_{xx}^{-1}&-v_{-}-\tfrac{1}{2a}\partial_{xx}^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad v_{\pm}\triangleq\pm a+\varepsilon r_{\pm},

(3.17)

with
{\mathcal{J}}
as in (
3.5
). At the equilibrium
(r_{+},r_{-})=(0,0)
, this becomes

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{M}_{0}(a)\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathbf{M}_{0}(a)\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}a-\tfrac{1}{2a}\partial_{xx}^{-1}&\tfrac{1}{2a}\partial_{xx}^{-1}\vskip 2.84544pt\\
\tfrac{1}{2a}\partial_{xx}^{-1}&a-\tfrac{1}{2a}\partial_{xx}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.18)

Expanding
\rho_{\pm}
in Fourier series

\rho_{\pm}(t,x)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\rho_{j}^{\pm}(t)\mathbf{e}_{j}(x),

the system (
3.18
) is equivalent to the following countably many linear autonomous differential systems

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{j}^{+}\\
\rho_{j}^{-}\end{pmatrix}=M_{j}(a)\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{j}^{+}\\
\rho_{j}^{-}\end{pmatrix},\qquad M_{j}(a)\triangleq\frac{{\rm i}j}{|j|}A_{j}(a),\qquad A_{j}(a)\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}-a|j|-\tfrac{1}{2a|j|}&\tfrac{1}{2a|j|}\\
-\tfrac{1}{2a|j|}&a|j|+\tfrac{1}{2a|j|}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.19)

The characteristic polynomial of the matrix
A_{j}(a)
writes

\displaystyle\chi_{A_{j}(a)}

\displaystyle=\Big(X+\big(a|j|+\tfrac{1}{2a|j|}\big)\Big)\Big(X-\big(a|j|+\tfrac{1}{2a|j|}\big)\Big)+\tfrac{1}{4a^{2}|j|^{2}}

\displaystyle=X^{2}-a^{2}|j|^{2}-1

\displaystyle=\Big(X-\sqrt{a^{2}|j|^{2}+1}\Big)\Big(X+\sqrt{a^{2}|j|^{2}+1}\Big).

Hence, the matrix
M_{j}(a)
is diagonalizable with simple pure imaginary eigenvalues
\pm{\rm i}\Omega_{j}(a)
defined by

\Omega_{j}(a)\triangleq\tfrac{j}{|j|}\sqrt{a^{2}|j|^{2}+1}.

(3.20)

In the sequel, refering the reader to the general discussion on pseudo-differential operators in Appendix
A.4.1
, we shall consider the following corresponding Fourier multiplier
\Omega(a,D)
acting on the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
associated with the following symbol

\Omega(a,\xi)\triangleq\chi(\xi)\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\sqrt{a^{2}\xi^{2}+1},\qquad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*},\quad\Omega(a,j)=\Omega_{j}(a),

(3.21)

where
\chi\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
is an even, positive
C^{\infty}
cut-off function such that

\chi(\xi)=0\ \textnormal{ if }\ |\xi|\leqslant\tfrac{1}{3}\,,\quad\chi(\xi)=1\ \textnormal{ if }\ |\xi|\geqslant\tfrac{2}{3}\,,\quad\partial_{\xi}\chi(\xi)>0\ \ \forall\,\xi\in(\tfrac{1}{3},\tfrac{2}{3}).

(3.22)

Before going further into the analysis of the linearized operator, let us first discuss some basic properties of the equilibrium eigenvalues (
3.20
) and their associated Fourier multiplier
\Omega(a,D).

Lemma 3.1
.

For any
a\in(a_{0},a_{1})
, the frequencies
\Omega_{j}(a)
in (
3.20
) and their associated operator
\Omega(a,D)
in (
3.21
) enjoy the following properties:

(i)

For any
\xi\in\mathbb{R},
we have
\Omega(a,-\xi)=-\Omega(a,\xi)
;

(ii)

The sequence
\big(\Omega_{j}(a)\big)_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is strictly increasing;

(iii)

For any
j\in\mathbb{Z},
we have

|\Omega_{j}(a)|\geqslant a_{0}|j|
;

(iv)

Let
M\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote

\mathfrak{N}_{M}\triangleq\max\big\{p\in\mathbb{N}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad 1-2p>-M\big\}=\begin{cases}\big\lfloor\tfrac{M+1}{2}\big\rfloor,&\textnormal{if }M\equiv 0\,\,[2],\\
\big\lfloor\tfrac{M+1}{2}\big\rfloor-1,&\textnormal{if }M\equiv 1\,\,[2].\end{cases}

(3.23)

Then, we have that
\Omega(a,D)\in OPS^{1}
(recall Definition
A.6
) with the following decomposition

{\rm i}\Omega(a,D)=a\partial_{x}+\sum_{p=1}^{\mathfrak{N}_{M}}\alpha_{p}\,a^{1-2p}\partial_{x}^{1-2p}+S_{M}(a,D),\qquad\alpha_{p}\triangleq\frac{(-1)^{p}}{p!}\prod_{m=0}^{p-1}(\tfrac{1}{2}-m),

(3.24)

where
S_{M}(a,D)
is a reversible Fourier multiplier in
OPS^{-M}
.

Proof.

The properties
(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
are readily obtained from (
3.20
)-(
3.21
).

We prove now item
(iv)
.
According to (
3.21
), we write for any
|\xi|\geqslant\tfrac{1}{3}

\Omega(a,\xi)=\chi(\xi)\Big[a\xi+a\xi\left(\sqrt{1+\tfrac{1}{a^{2}\xi^{2}}}-1\right)\Big]\triangleq\chi(\xi)\Big(a\xi+\mathtt{r}(a,\xi)\Big).

(3.25)

Note that it is sufficient to restrict the discussion to
|\xi|\geqslant\tfrac{1}{3}
because the cut of function
\chi
vanishes below. Clearly
-\mathtt{r}(a,-\xi)=\mathtt{r}(a,\xi)>0.

In particular, the remainder
\mathtt{r}(a,\xi)
is explicitly given, for a fixed
M\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, by

\mathtt{r}(a,\xi)=\sum_{p=1}^{\mathfrak{N}_{M}}\frac{c_{p}}{a^{2p-1}\xi^{2p-1}}+\mathtt{s}_{M}(a,\xi),

(3.26)

where

c_{p}\triangleq\frac{1}{p!}\prod_{m=0}^{p-1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}-m\right)

and

\displaystyle\mathtt{s}_{M}(a,\xi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{\mathfrak{N}_{M}!(a\xi)^{2\mathfrak{N}_{M}}}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\tau)^{\mathfrak{N}_{M}}\Big(\partial_{z}^{\mathfrak{N}_{M}+1}\sqrt{1+z}\Big)|_{z=\frac{\tau}{(a\xi)^{2}}}{\rm d}\tau

(3.27)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{N}_{M}!(a\xi)^{2\mathfrak{N}_{M}}}\prod_{m=0}^{\mathfrak{N}_{M}}(\tfrac{1}{2}-m)\int_{0}^{1}(1-\tau)^{\mathfrak{N}_{M}}\left(1+\frac{\tau}{a^{2}\xi^{2}}\right)^{-\tfrac{1}{2}-\mathfrak{N}_{M}}{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle=a\xi\left(\mathfrak{N}_{M}+1\right)c_{\mathfrak{N}_{M}+1}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\tau)^{\mathfrak{N}_{M}}\left(1+\tau a^{2}\xi^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mathfrak{N}_{M}}d\tau.

Therefore,

\displaystyle{\rm i}\Omega(a,D)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{Op}\big(\chi(\xi)a{\rm i}\xi\big)+\textnormal{Op}\left(\chi(\xi)\sum_{p=1}^{\mathfrak{N}_{M}}\frac{(-1)^{p}c_{p}}{a^{2p-1}({\rm i}\xi)^{2p-1}}\right)+\textnormal{Op}\big(\chi(\xi){\rm i}\mathtt{s}_{M}(a,D)\big)

\displaystyle=a\partial_{x}+\sum_{p=1}^{\mathfrak{N}_{M}}(-1)^{p}c_{p}a^{1-2p}\partial_{x}^{1-2p}+S_{M}(a,D),\qquad S_{M}(a,D)\triangleq\textnormal{Op}\big(\chi(\xi){\rm i}\mathtt{s}_{M}(a,\xi)\big).

(3.28)

Clearly, with the last expression in (
3.27
), we get
\mathtt{s}_{M}(a,-\xi)=-\mathtt{s}_{M}(a,\xi)
, which implies the reversibility of
S_{M}(a,D)
by virtue of Proposition
A.3
-6. Now, let us prove that
S_{M}(a,D)
is indeed of order
-M.

Note that, from the last expression in (
3.27
), one readily has that for any
(k,\ell)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,

\sup_{|\xi|\geqslant\frac{1}{3}\atop a\in[a_{0},a_{1}]}\langle\xi\rangle^{\ell+M}\left|\partial_{a}^{k}\partial_{\xi}^{\ell}\mathtt{s}_{M}(a,\xi)\right|\leqslant C(a_{0},k,\ell)<\infty.

Since
\chi
is constant on
\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:|\xi|\geqslant\tfrac{2}{3}\},
then the Leibniz rule combined with the previous estimate allows to conclude the proof of the claim.
∎

Consider the symplectic (and thus invertible) transfer matrix
Q_{j}(a)
defined by

Q_{j}(a)\triangleq\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-b_{j}^{2}(a)}}\begin{pmatrix}1&b_{j}(a)\\
b_{j}(a)&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad b_{j}(a)\triangleq\frac{1}{2a|j|\left(\sqrt{a^{2}j^{2}+1}+a|j|+\frac{1}{2a|j|}\right)}\in(0,1).

(3.29)

Note that
Q_{j}(a)\in SL_{2}(\mathbb{R})=Sp_{2}(\mathbb{R}).
In particular it is invertible with inverse given by

Q_{j}^{-1}(a)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-b_{j}^{2}(a)}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-b_{j}(a)\\
-b_{j}(a)&1\end{pmatrix}.

(3.30)

Moreover, one checks that

\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*},\quad Q_{j}^{-1}(a)M_{j}(a)Q_{j}(a)=-\begin{pmatrix}{\rm i}\Omega_{j}(a)&0\\
0&\overline{{\rm i}\Omega_{j}(a)}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.31)

Hence, one solves the linear differential systems (
3.19
) and obtains the following structure of solutions to (
3.18
)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}A_{j}e^{{\rm i}(jx-\Omega_{j}(a)t)}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
b_{j}(a)\end{pmatrix}+B_{j}e^{{\rm i}(jx+\Omega_{j}(a)t)}\begin{pmatrix}b_{j}(a)\\
1\end{pmatrix},

where
A_{j}=\overline{A_{-j}}
and
B_{j}=\overline{B_{-j}}.
These solutions are either periodic, quasi-periodic or almost-periodic. In particular, the reversible solutions, namely solutions satisfying
\rho_{\pm}(t,x)=\rho_{\pm}(-t,-x)
, have the form

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}(t,x)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}{\widetilde{A}}_{j}\cos\big(\Omega_{j}(a)t-jx\big)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
b_{j}(a)\end{pmatrix}+{\widetilde{B}}_{j}\cos\big(\Omega_{j}(a)t+jx\big)\begin{pmatrix}b_{j}(a)\\
1\end{pmatrix},

(3.32)

with
{\widetilde{A}}_{j},{\widetilde{B}}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}.

3.3
Degenerate KAM theory and linear solutions

Our next task is to check the so called
Rüssmann transversality conditions
for the equilibrium frequency vectors. We refer to Lemma
3.3
for a complete statement. Such conditions are required for measuring some sets of parameters in the sequel.

We fix
(d_{+},d_{-})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
and consider two finite sets of distinct elements

\mathbb{S}_{+}\triangleq\big\{j_{1}^{+},\ldots,j_{d_{+}}^{+}\big\}\subset\mathbb{N}^{*},\qquad\mathbb{S}_{-}\triangleq\big\{j_{1}^{-},\ldots,j_{d_{-}}^{-}\big\}\subset\mathbb{N}^{*},

(3.33)

with

|\mathbb{S}_{+}|=d_{+},\qquad|\mathbb{S}_{-}|=d_{-},\qquad\mathbb{S}_{+}\cap\mathbb{S}_{-}=\varnothing.

(3.34)

In what follows, we also use the following notations

d\triangleq d_{+}+d_{-},\qquad\mathbb{S}\triangleq\mathbb{S}_{+}\sqcup\mathbb{S}_{-}\triangleq\big\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{d}\big\}\subset\mathbb{N}^{*},\qquad\mathbb{S}_{0}\triangleq\mathbb{S}\cup\{0\}.

(3.35)

For later purposes, we shall also introduce the symmetric tangential set

\forall\,\kappa\in\{-,+\},\quad\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\kappa}\triangleq\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}\sqcup(-\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}),\qquad\overline{\mathbb{S}}\triangleq\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{+}\sqcup\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-},\qquad\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}\triangleq\mathbb{S}_{0}\sqcup(-\mathbb{S}_{0}).

(3.36)

Then, we define the
equilibrium frequency vector
(also called
unperturbed frequency vector
later on)

\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\triangleq\Big(\Omega_{j_{1}^{+}}(a),\ldots,\Omega_{j_{d_{+}}^{+}}(a),-\Omega_{j_{1}^{-}}(a),\ldots,-\Omega_{j_{d_{-}}^{-}}(a)\Big)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(3.37)

Lemma 3.2

(Non-degeneracy)
.

The following vector functions

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\qquad a\mapsto\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)-\vec{\jmath}\,a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\qquad a\mapsto\big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a),a\big)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1},

(3.38)

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\omega_{\rm Eq}(a),\Omega_{j}(a)\big)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1},\quad j\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{S}}},

(3.39)

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\omega_{\rm Eq}(a),\Omega_{j}(a),\Omega_{j^{\prime}}(a)\big)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+2},\quad j,j^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{S}}},\quad|j|\neq|j^{\prime}|,

(3.40)

are non-degenerate on
[a_{0},a_{1}]
, that is

\displaystyle\forall\,c\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(\forall\,a\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\quad\big\langle c,\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}=0\Big)\quad\Rightarrow\quad c=0,

(3.41)

\displaystyle{\forall\,c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}},

\displaystyle{\qquad\Big(\forall\,a\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\quad\big\langle c,\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)-\vec{\jmath}\,a\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}=0\Big)\quad\Rightarrow\quad c=0,}

(3.42)

\displaystyle\forall\,c\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1},

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(\forall\,a\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\quad\big\langle c,\big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a),a\big)\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}=0\Big)\quad\Rightarrow\quad c=0,

(3.43)

\displaystyle\forall\,c\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1},

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(\forall\,a\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\quad\big\langle c,\big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a),\Omega_{j}(a)\big)\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}=0\Big)\quad\Rightarrow\quad c=0,

(3.44)

\displaystyle\forall\,c\in\mathbb{R}^{d+2},

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(\forall\,a\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\quad\big\langle c,\big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a),\Omega_{j}(a),\Omega_{j^{\prime}}(a)\big)\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d+2}}=0\Big)\quad\Rightarrow\quad c=0.

(3.45)

Proof.

We start by proving that
a\mapsto\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)
is non-degenerate.
We decompose
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
into
c=(c_{+},c_{-})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{-}}
, then, up to replacing
c_{-}
by
-c_{-}
, we see that we can assume that
d_{-}=0
(i.e.
d_{+}=d
) and prove that the vector function
a\mapsto\big(\Omega_{j}(a)\big)_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{+}}
is non-degenerate on
[a_{0},a_{1}]
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Until the end of the proof, we simplify the notation into
j_{k}^{+}\triangleq j_{k}
for
k\in\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket.
Assume that there exists
(c_{1},\ldots,c_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\forall\,a\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\quad\sum_{k=1}^{d}c_{k}\sqrt{a^{2}j_{k}^{2}+1}=0.

This equation is equivalent to

\forall\,x\in\big[a_{0}^{2},a_{1}^{2}\big],\quad\sum_{k=1}^{d}c_{k}\sqrt{xj_{k}^{2}+1}=0.

By analytic continuation principle, we extend the previous relation to the range
x\in\left(-\tfrac{1}{j_{d}^{2}},\infty\right).
For any
n\in\llbracket 0,d-1\rrbracket,
differentiating the previous relation
n
times with respect to
x
and evaluating at
x=0
yields

\forall\,n\in\llbracket 0,d-1\rrbracket,\quad\sum_{k=1}^{d}c_{k}j_{k}^{2n}=0.

This is a Vandermonde invertible system since from (
3.34
) all the
j_{k}^{2}
are distinct. Hence,
c_{1}=\ldots=c_{d}=0,
which achieves the proof of the first point.

The second and the third vector functions are obtained by similar techniques, noting that
\partial_{\alpha}^{n}a=0
for any
n\geqslant 2
, whereas the last two cases follow from the first, by extending
\omega_{\rm Eq}(a)
with the extra components
(\Omega_{j}(a))
and
(\Omega_{j}(a),\Omega_{j^{\prime}}(a))
and arguing as before. This ends the proof of Lemma
3.2
.
∎

From the non-degeneracy of the vectors in Lemma
3.2
, we deduce the following momentum preserving Rüssmann transversality conditions.

Lemma 3.3

(Equilibrium transversality)
.

Let
{\mathbb{S}}_{+}
,
{\mathbb{S}}_{-}
,
(d_{+},d_{-})
as in (
3.33
), (
3.34
) and (
3.35
). We define the velocity vector
\vec{\jmath}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
as

\displaystyle\vec{\jmath}

\displaystyle\triangleq\big(j_{1}^{+},\ldots,j_{d_{+}}^{+},j_{1}^{-},\ldots,j_{d_{-}}^{-}\big)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

(3.46)

There exist
\rho_{0}>0
and
q_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the following hold true.

(i)

For all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,

\inf_{a\in[a_{0},a_{1}]}\max_{k\in\llbracket 0,q_{0}\rrbracket}\Big|\partial_{a}^{k}\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell\Big|\geqslant\rho_{0}\langle\ell\rangle.

(ii)

For all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

\inf_{a\in[a_{0},a_{1}]}\max_{k\in\llbracket 0,q_{0}\rrbracket}\Big|\partial_{a}^{k}\big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)-\vec{\jmath}\,a\big)\cdot\ell\Big|\geqslant\rho_{0}\langle\ell\rangle.

(iii)

Let
\kappa\in\{-,+\}.
For all
(\ell,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times(\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\kappa})
with
\vec{\jmath}\cdot\ell+j=0,

\inf_{a\in[a_{0},a_{1}]}\max_{k\in\llbracket 0,q_{0}\rrbracket}\Big|\partial_{a}^{k}\big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell+\kappa\Omega_{j}(a)\big)\Big|\geqslant\rho_{0}\langle\ell\rangle.

(iv)

Let
\kappa\in\{-,+\}.
For all
(\ell,j,j^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times(\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\kappa})^{2}
with
(\ell,j,j^{\prime})\neq(0,j,j)
and
\vec{\jmath}\cdot\ell+j-j^{\prime}=0,

\inf_{a\in[a_{0},a_{1}]}\max_{k\in\llbracket 0,q_{0}\rrbracket}\Big|\partial_{a}^{k}\big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell+\kappa\Omega_{j}(a)-\kappa\Omega_{j^{\prime}}(a)\big)\Big|\geqslant\rho_{0}\langle\ell\rangle.

(v)

For all
(\ell,j,j^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times(\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{+})\times(\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-})
with
\vec{\jmath}\cdot\ell+j-j^{\prime}=0,

\inf_{a\in[a_{0},a_{1}]}\max_{k\in\llbracket 0,q_{0}\rrbracket}\Big|\partial_{a}^{k}\big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell+\Omega_{j}(a)-\Omega_{j^{\prime}}(a)\big)\Big|\geqslant\rho_{0}\langle\ell\rangle.

Proof.

(i)
We argue by contradiction. Hence, we assume the existence of sequences
(a_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in[a_{0},a_{1}]^{\mathbb{N}}
and
(\ell_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\})^{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\forall\,m\geqslant k,\quad\Big|\partial_{a}^{k}\Big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\tfrac{\ell_{m}}{|\ell_{m}|}\Big)\Big|_{a=a_{m}}\Big|<\frac{1}{1+m}.

(3.47)

By compactness, using that
\ell_{m}\neq 0
, up to subsequences we have the following convergences

\lim_{m\to\infty}a_{m}=\overline{a}\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{\ell_{m}}{|\ell_{m}|}=\overline{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{*}

(3.48)

Therefore, taking
m\to\infty
in (
3.47
), we get

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\partial_{a}^{k}\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(\overline{a})\cdot\overline{\ell}=0.

We deduce that the analytic mapping
a\mapsto\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\overline{\ell}
is identically zero, which is in contradiction with Lemma
3.2
since
\overline{\ell}\neq 0
.

(ii)
As in the previous point, we argue by contradiction, assuming the existence of sequences
(a_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in[a_{0},a_{1}]^{\mathbb{N}}
and
(\ell_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\})^{\mathbb{N}}

such that

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\forall\,m\geqslant k,\quad\Big|\partial_{a}^{k}\Big(\big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)-\vec{\jmath}\,a\big)\cdot\tfrac{\ell_{m}}{|\ell_{m}|}\Big)\Big|_{a=a_{m}}\Big|<\frac{1}{1+m}\cdot

(3.49)

Once again, a compactness argument implies, up to subsequences, the same convergences as in (
3.48
).
Passing to the limit
m\to\infty
in (
3.49
), we obtain

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\partial_{a}^{k}\Big(\big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)-\vec{\jmath}\,a\big)\cdot\overline{\ell}\Big)\Big|_{a=\overline{a}}=0.

Hence, the analytic mapping
a\mapsto\big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)-\vec{\jmath}\,a\big)\cdot\overline{\ell}
is identically zero, which contradicts Lemma
3.2
since
\overline{\ell}\neq 0
.

(iii)
First, if
\ell=0
, then, by the momentum condition
\vec{\jmath}\cdot\ell+j=0
, we have
j=0
, which is excluded.
Therefore, we restrict the discussion to the case
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}.
In addition, by triangle inequality and Lemma
3.1
-
(iii)
, we get for any
a\in[a_{0},a_{1}],

\displaystyle\big|\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell+\kappa\Omega_{j}(a)\big|

\displaystyle\geqslant|\Omega_{j}(a)|-\big|\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell\big|

\displaystyle\geqslant a_{0}|j|-C|\ell|

\displaystyle\geqslant|\ell|,

provided that
|j|\geqslant C_{1}|\ell|
with
C_{1}\triangleq\tfrac{C+1}{a_{0}}.
Therefore, we shall restrict our discussion to the indices satisfying

\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\},\qquad j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\kappa},\qquad|j|<C_{1}|\ell|.

(3.50)

As in the first point, we argue by contradiction, assuming the existence of sequences
(a_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in[a_{0},a_{1}]^{\mathbb{N}}
,
(\ell_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\})^{\mathbb{N}}
and
(j_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\kappa})^{\mathbb{N}}
such that

|j_{m}|<C_{1}|\ell_{m}|,\qquad\vec{\jmath}\cdot\ell_{m}+j_{m}=0

(3.51)

and

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\forall\,m\geqslant k,\quad\Big|\partial_{a}^{k}\Big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\tfrac{\ell_{m}}{|\ell_{m}|}+\kappa\tfrac{\Omega_{j_{m}}(a)}{|\ell_{m}|}\Big)\Big|_{a=a_{m}}\Big|<\frac{1}{1+m}\cdot

(3.52)

Once again, using also (
3.51
), a compactness argument implies that, up to subsequences,

\lim_{m\to\infty}a_{m}=\overline{a}\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\qquad\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{\ell_{m}}{|\ell_{m}|}=\overline{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{*}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{j_{m}}{|\ell_{m}|}=\overline{d}\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.53)

We distinguish two cases.

Case 1:
(\ell_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is bounded.
By (
3.51
), also the sequence
(j_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is bounded. Therefore, by compactness, up to subsequences, we have that both sequences of integers satisfy

\ell_{m}=\ell_{\infty}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\},\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad j_{m}=j_{\infty}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\kappa}\qquad\textnormal{definitely for any}\quad m\gg 1\quad\textnormal{large enough}.

Hence, taking the limit
m\to\infty
in (
3.52
) and (
3.51
), we obtain

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\partial_{a}^{k}\Big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell_{\infty}+\kappa\Omega_{j_{\infty}}(a)\Big)\Big|_{a=\overline{a}}=0\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\vec{\jmath}\cdot\ell_{\infty}+j_{\infty}=0.

(3.54)

Consequently, the analytic mapping
a\mapsto\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell_{\infty}+\kappa\Omega_{j_{\infty}}(a)
is identically zero, namely

\forall\,a\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\quad\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)\cdot\ell_{\infty}+\kappa\Omega_{j_{\infty}}(a)=0.

(3.55)

Now, we distinguish two subcases.

•

Subcase 1.1:
j_{\infty}\not\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-\kappa}.
In this case
j_{\infty}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{S}}}
and we reach a contradiction with Lemma
3.2
since
(\ell_{\infty},\kappa)\neq 0
.

•

Subcase 1.2:
j_{\infty}\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-\kappa}.
There exists
k_{0}\in\llbracket 1,d_{-\kappa}\rrbracket
such that
j_{\infty}=\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty})j_{k_{0}}^{-\kappa},
where
\mathtt{sgn}
is the sign function. We denote

\ell_{\infty}=(\ell_{\infty}^{[+,1]},\ldots,\ell_{\infty}^{[+,d_{+}]},\ell_{\infty}^{[-,1]},\ldots,\ell_{\infty}^{[-,d_{-}]}).

(3.56)

Then, recalling (
3.37
), the identity in (
3.55
) reads

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)\cdot\ell_{\infty}+\kappa\Omega_{j_{\infty}}(a)

(3.57)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\llbracket 1,d_{\kappa}\rrbracket}\ell_{\infty}^{[\kappa,k]}\Omega_{j_{k}^{\kappa}}(a)+\sum_{k\in\llbracket 1,d_{-\kappa}\rrbracket\setminus\{k_{0}\}}\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k]}\Omega_{j_{k}^{-\kappa}}(a)+\big(-\kappa\,\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}]}+\kappa\,\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty})\big)\Omega_{j_{k_{0}}^{-\kappa}}(a).

Combining (
3.55
), (
3.57
) and Lemma
3.2
, we obtain

\forall\,k\in\llbracket 1,d_{\kappa}\rrbracket,\quad\ell_{\infty}^{[\kappa,k]}=0\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\forall\,k\,\in\llbracket 1,d_{-\kappa}\rrbracket\setminus\{k_{0}\},\quad\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k]}=0

and

-\kappa\,\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}]}+\kappa\,\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty})=0,\qquad\textnormal{i.e.}\qquad\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}]}=\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty}).

Inserting this into the momentum condition (
3.54
), we get

0=\vec{\jmath}\cdot\ell_{\infty}+j_{\infty}=j_{k_{0}}^{-\kappa}\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}]}+\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty})j_{k_{0}}^{-\kappa}=2j_{\infty}.

This is a contradiction with
j_{\infty}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}.

Case 2:
(\ell_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is unbounded.
Without loss of generality, up to subsequences we assume

\lim_{m\to\infty}|\ell_{m}|=\infty.

According to the Lemma
3.1
-
(iv)
, we write

\Omega_{j}(a)=aj+\mathtt{r}_{j}(a),

(3.58)

with
\mathtt{r}_{j}(a)
satisfying
\mathtt{r}_{-j}(a)=-\mathtt{r}_{j}(a)
and

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\sup_{a\in[a_{0},a_{1}]\atop j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}|j||\partial_{a}^{k}\mathtt{r}_{j}(a)|\leqslant C(a_{0},a_{1},k).

(3.59)

Then, we write

\frac{\Omega_{j_{m}}(a)}{|\ell_{m}|}=\frac{aj_{m}}{|\ell_{m}|}+\frac{\mathtt{r}_{j_{m}}(a)}{|\ell_{m}|}\cdot

Hence, whatever the fact that
(j_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is bounded or not, by (
3.59
) we have

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{\partial_{a}^{k}\mathtt{r}_{j_{m}}(\overline{a})}{|\ell_{m}|}=0.

Thus, taking the limit
m\to\infty
in (
3.52
) and recalling (
3.53
), we obtain

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\partial_{a}^{k}\Big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\overline{\ell}+\overline{d}\kappa\,a\Big)\Big|_{a=\overline{a}}=0.

Consequently, the analytic mapping
a\mapsto\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\overline{\ell}+\overline{d}\kappa\,a
is identically zero, namely

\forall\,a\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\quad\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)\cdot\overline{\ell}+\overline{d}\kappa\,a=0,

which also leads to a contradiction with Lemma
3.2
since
(\overline{\ell},\overline{d})\neq 0
.

(iv)

First, if
\ell=0
, then, by the momentum condition
\vec{\jmath}\cdot\ell+j-j^{\prime}=0
, we have
j=j^{\prime}
, which is excluded. Therefore, we restrict the discussion to
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
.
By triangle inequality and (
3.58
)-(
3.59
), we have

\displaystyle\big|\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell+\kappa\Omega_{j}(a)-\kappa\Omega_{j^{\prime}}(a)\big|

\displaystyle\geqslant a|j-j^{\prime}|-|\mathtt{r}_{j}(a)|-|\mathtt{r}_{j^{\prime}}(a)|-\big|\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell\big|

\displaystyle\geqslant a_{0}|j-j^{\prime}|-C|\ell|

\displaystyle\geqslant|\ell|,

provided that
|j-j^{\prime}|\geqslant C_{2}|\ell|
with
C_{2}\triangleq\tfrac{C+1}{a_{0}}.
Therefore, we shall restrict our discussion to the indices satisfying

\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\},\qquad j,j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\kappa},\qquad|j-j^{\prime}|<C_{2}|\ell|.

As in the first point, we argue by contradiction assuming the existence of sequences
(a_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in[a_{0},a_{1}]^{\mathbb{N}}
,
(\ell_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\})^{\mathbb{N}}
and
(j_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}},(j^{\prime}_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\kappa})^{\mathbb{N}}
such that

|j_{m}-j^{\prime}_{m}|<C_{2}|\ell_{m}|,\qquad\vec{\jmath}\cdot\ell_{m}+j_{m}-j_{m}^{\prime}=0,\qquad j_{m}\neq j^{\prime}_{m},

(3.60)

and

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\forall\,m\geqslant k,\quad\Big|\partial_{a}^{k}\Big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\tfrac{\ell_{m}}{|\ell_{m}|}+\kappa\,\tfrac{\Omega_{j_{m}}(a)-\Omega_{j^{\prime}_{m}}(a)}{|\ell_{m}|}\Big)\Big|_{a=a_{m}}\Big|<\frac{1}{1+m}\cdot

(3.61)

By compactness, up to subsequences, we have the following convergences

\lim_{m\to\infty}a_{m}=\overline{a}\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\qquad\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{\ell_{m}}{|\ell_{m}|}=\overline{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{*}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{j_{m}-j^{\prime}_{m}}{|\ell_{m}|}=\overline{d}\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.62)

We distinguish four cases.

Case 1:
(\ell_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
(j_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
(j^{\prime}_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
are bounded.
Therefore, by compactness, up to subsequences, we have that these sequences of integers satisfy

\ell_{m}=\ell_{\infty}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\},\qquad j_{m}=j_{\infty}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\kappa}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad j^{\prime}_{m}=j^{\prime}_{\infty}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\kappa}

definitely for any
m\gg 1
large enough. In particular, by (
3.60
), we have that

\vec{\jmath}\cdot\ell_{\infty}+j_{\infty}-j^{\prime}_{\infty}=0\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad j_{\infty}\neq j_{\infty}^{\prime}.

(3.63)

Passing to the limit
m\to\infty
in (
3.61
), we infer

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\partial_{a}^{k}\Big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell_{\infty}+\kappa\Omega_{j_{\infty}}(a)-\kappa\Omega_{j^{\prime}_{\infty}}(a)\Big)\Big|_{a=\overline{a}}=0.

Hence, the analytic mapping
a\mapsto\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\ell_{\infty}+\kappa\Omega_{j_{\infty}}(a)-\kappa\Omega_{j^{\prime}_{\infty}}(a)
is identically zero, namely

\forall\,a\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\quad\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)\cdot\ell_{\infty}+\kappa\Omega_{j_{\infty}}(a)-\kappa\Omega_{j_{\infty}^{\prime}}(a)=0.

(3.64)

We further need to seperate three cases.

•

Subcase 1.1:
j_{\infty},j_{\infty}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-\kappa}.
In this case
j_{\infty},j_{\infty}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{S}}}.
If
|j_{\infty}|\neq|j_{\infty}^{\prime}|
, then we immediately reach a contradiction with Lemma
3.2
. Otherwise, in view of (
3.63
), we have
j_{\infty}=-j_{\infty}^{\prime}
and (
3.64
) becomes

\forall\,a\in[a_{0},a_{1}],\quad\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)\cdot\ell_{\infty}+2\kappa\Omega_{j_{\infty}}(a)=0,

which, combined with the fact that
j_{\infty}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}},
leads also to a contradiction with Lemma
3.2
since
(\ell_{\infty},2\kappa)\neq 0
.

•

Subcase 1.2:
j_{\infty},j_{\infty}^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-\kappa}.
There exists
(k_{0},k_{0}^{\prime})\in\llbracket 1,d_{-\kappa}\rrbracket^{2}
such that
j_{\infty}=\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty})j_{k_{0}}^{-\kappa}
and
j_{\infty}^{\prime}=\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty}^{\prime})j_{k_{0}^{\prime}}^{-\kappa}
. We denote
\ell_{\infty}
as in (
3.56
). Then, recalling (
3.37
), the identity in (
3.64
) reads

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)\cdot\ell_{\infty}+\kappa\Omega_{j_{\infty}}(a)-\kappa\Omega_{j_{\infty}^{\prime}}(a)

(3.65)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\llbracket 1,d_{\kappa}\rrbracket}\ell_{\infty}^{[\kappa,k]}\Omega_{j_{k}^{\kappa}}(a)+\sum_{k\in\llbracket 1,d_{-\kappa}\rrbracket\setminus\{k_{0},k_{0}^{\prime}\}}\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k]}\Omega_{j_{k}^{-\kappa}}(a)

\displaystyle+\big(-\kappa\,\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}]}+\kappa\,\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty})\big)\Omega_{j_{k_{0}}^{-\kappa}}(a)+\big(-\kappa\,\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}^{\prime}]}-\kappa\,\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty}^{\prime})\big)\Omega_{j_{k_{0}^{\prime}}^{-\kappa}}(a).

Combining (
3.64
), (
3.65
) and Lemma
3.2
, we obtain

\forall\,k\in\llbracket 1,d_{\kappa}\rrbracket,\quad\ell_{\infty}^{[\kappa,k]}=0\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\forall\,k\in\llbracket 1,d_{-\kappa}\rrbracket\setminus\{k_{0},k_{0}^{\prime}\},\quad\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}]}=0

as well as

-\kappa\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}]}+\kappa\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty})=0\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad-\kappa\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}^{\prime}]}-\kappa\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty}^{\prime})=0.

i.e.

\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}]}=\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty})\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}^{\prime}]}=-\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty}^{\prime}).

Inserting this into the momentum condition (
3.63
), we get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\vec{\jmath}\cdot\ell_{\infty}+j_{\infty}-j_{\infty}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=j_{k_{0}}^{-\kappa}\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}]}+j_{k_{0}^{\prime}}^{-\kappa}\ell_{\infty}^{[-\kappa,k_{0}^{\prime}]}+\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty})j_{k_{0}}^{-\kappa}-\mathtt{sgn}(j_{\infty}^{\prime})j_{k_{0}^{\prime}}^{-\kappa}

\displaystyle=2(j_{\infty}-j_{\infty}^{\prime}).

This is a contradiction with the second identity in (
3.63
).

•

Subcase 1.3: (
j_{\infty}\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-\kappa}
and
j_{\infty}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-\kappa}
) or (
j_{\infty}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-\kappa}
and
j_{\infty}^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-\kappa}
).
This case is a combination of the previous subcases, and its analysis is therefore omitted.

Case 2:
(\ell_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is bounded, with
(j_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
bounded and
(j^{\prime}_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
unbounded (or viceversa).
This case is actually excluded, as it violates the momentum condition (
3.60
) in the limit
m\to\infty
.

Case 3:
(\ell_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is bounded and
(j_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
(j^{\prime}_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
are unbounded.
Without loss of generality, up to subsequences we have

\lim_{m\to\infty}j_{m}=\infty\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\lim_{m\to\infty}j^{\prime}_{m}=\infty,

Using the splitting (
3.58
), we write

\frac{\Omega_{j_{m}}(a)-\Omega_{j_{m}^{\prime}}(a)}{|\ell_{m}|}=\frac{a(j_{m}-j_{m}^{\prime})}{|\ell_{m}|}+\frac{{\mathtt{r}}_{j_{m}}(a)}{|\ell_{m}|}-\frac{{\mathtt{r}}_{j_{m}^{\prime}}(a)}{|\ell_{m}|},

(3.66)

According to (
3.59
), we have

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\lim_{m\to\infty}\partial_{a}^{k}\mathtt{r}_{j_{m}}(\overline{a})=\lim_{m\to\infty}\partial_{a}^{k}\mathtt{r}_{j^{\prime}_{m}}(\overline{a})=0.

Thus, taking
m\to\infty
in (
3.61
) and using (
3.62
) imply

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\partial_{a}^{k}\Big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\overline{\ell}+\overline{d}\kappa\,a\Big)\Big|_{a=\overline{a}}=0,

Therefore, the analytic function
a\mapsto\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)\cdot\overline{\ell}+\overline{d}\kappa\,a
with
(\overline{\ell},\overline{d})\neq 0
vanishes identically, which provides a contradiction as previously.

Case 4:
(\ell_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is unbounded.
In this case, we have the convergence

\lim_{m\to\infty}|\ell_{m}|=\infty.

Recall from (
3.59
), that the sequence
({\mathtt{r}}_{j}(a))_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
and its derivatives are uniformly bounded. Therefore, using one more time the splitting (
3.66
), passing to the limit
m\to\infty
in (
3.61
) and using (
3.62
), we deduce

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\partial_{a}^{k}\Big(\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\cdot\overline{\ell}+\overline{d}\kappa\,a\Big)\Big|_{a=\overline{a}}=0,

Therefore, the analytic function
a\mapsto\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)\cdot\overline{\ell}+\overline{d}\kappa\,a
with
(\overline{\ell},\overline{d})\neq 0
vanishes identically, which gives a contradiction with Lemma
3.2
.

(v)
This point is proved similarly to the previous one so we omit it. This concludes the proof of Lemma
3.3
.
∎

We conclude this section with the following lemma stating the existence of traveling quasi-periodic solutions to the linear system (
3.18
) obtained exciting only a finite number of Fourier modes.

Lemma 3.4
.

(Traveling quasi-periodic solutions to the equilibrium linearized system)

Let
{\mathbb{S}}_{+}
,
{\mathbb{S}}_{-}
as in (
3.33
), (
3.34
).
Then, there exists a set
\mathscr{C}_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}\subset(a_{0},a_{1})
of full measure in
(a_{0},a_{1})
such that, for any
a\in\mathscr{C}_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}
, the system (
3.18
) admits the following reversible traveling quasi-periodic solutions

\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}(t,x)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{+}}A_{j}\cos\big(\Omega_{j}(a)t-jx\big)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
b_{j}(a)\end{pmatrix}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{-}}B_{j}\cos\big(\Omega_{j}(a)t+jx\big)\begin{pmatrix}b_{j}(a)\\
1\end{pmatrix},

(3.67)

with
(A_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{+}},(B_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{-}}\subset\mathbb{R}.
Such solutions are associated with the frequency vector
\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)
as in (
3.37
) and velocity vector
\vec{\jmath}
defined in (
3.46
).

Proof.

The form of the solution (
3.67
) has been computed in (
3.32
) at the end of Section
3.2
. We are left to prove the measure estimate for the non-resonance conditions leading to quasi-periodicity.
Let us consider
d_{+}
and
d_{-}
as in (
3.34
). Fix
\tau\triangleq(d_{+}+d_{-})q_{0}
and define

\mathscr{C}_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}\triangleq\bigcup_{\gamma\in(0,1)}\mathscr{C}_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}^{\gamma},\qquad\mathscr{C}_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}^{\gamma}\triangleq\bigcap_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d_{+}+d_{-}}\setminus\{0\}}\Big\{a\in(a_{0},a_{1})\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad|\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)\cdot\ell|>\frac{\gamma}{\langle\ell\rangle^{\tau}}\Big\}.

Then, using
[
60
, Lem. 3.6]
(which is a modified version of the classical Rüssmann Lemma
[
89
, Thm. 17.1]
) together with Lemma
3.3
-
(i)
, we obtain for any
\gamma\in(0,1),

\displaystyle\big|(a_{0},a_{1})\setminus\mathscr{C}_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}^{\gamma}\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d_{+}+d_{-}}\setminus\{0\}}\left|\Big\{a\in(a_{0},a_{1})\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad|\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)\cdot\ell|\leqslant\frac{\gamma}{\langle\ell\rangle^{\tau}}\Big\}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d_{+}+d_{-}}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\ell\rangle^{-\frac{\tau+1}{q_{0}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}.

The convergence of the previous series is due to our choice of
\tau.
Hence,

a_{1}-a_{0}-C\gamma^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\leqslant|\mathscr{C}_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}^{\gamma}|\leqslant|\mathscr{C}_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}|\leqslant a_{1}-a_{0}.

Letting
\gamma\to 0
, we get that
\mathscr{C}_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}
is of full measure in
(a_{0},a_{1}).
Finally, observe that any choice of
a\in\mathscr{C}_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}
implies that
\omega_{\textnormal{\tiny{Eq}}}(a)
is non-resonant. This achieves the proof of Lemma
3.4
.
∎

4
Reformulation with embedded tori

As we have seen in Lemma
3.4
, there exist traveling quasi-periodic wave solutions at the linear level. Our aim now is to construct traveling quasi-periodic waves nearby such linear solutions for the full nonlinear system. Refering to the proof of Lemma
3.4
, small divisors/resonances appear. Consequently, one must employ a Nash-Moser scheme to construct the desired solutions. The aim of the present section is to introduce a suitable functional adapted to the implementation of the implicit function Nash-Moser theorem in Section
7
.
We achieve this in two steps: first we transform the Hamiltonian system through a suitable change of unknowns in order to put the equilibrium equation in normal form; then we introduce suitable action-angle-normal symplectic coordinates allowing to reformulate our problem in terms of embedded tori.

Normal form of the unperturbed linear term.

Note that the system (
3.5
) can be seen as a quasilinear perturbation of the equilibrium linear system (
3.18
). Indeed, we can write

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}r_{+}\\
r_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}{\mathcal{J}}\nabla{\mathcal{E}}(r)=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{M}_{0}\begin{pmatrix}r_{+}\\
r_{-}\end{pmatrix}+\varepsilon X_{P_{\mathcal{E}}}(r),\qquad X_{P_{\mathcal{E}}}(r)\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}-r_{+}\partial_{x}r_{+}\\
-r_{-}\partial_{x}r_{-}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.1)

Let us emphasize that the particular structure of the equations implies that the vector field
X_{P_{\mathcal{E}}}(r)
is independant of
\varepsilon.

Our first step is to perfom a symplectic change the unknown so that the new linearized system is in normal form. We define the matrix operator

\mathbf{Q}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}Q_{j}(a)\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{j}^{+}\\
\rho_{j}^{-}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{j},\qquad\textnormal{with inverse}\qquad\mathbf{Q}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}Q_{j}^{-1}(a)\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{j}^{+}\\
\rho_{j}^{-}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{j},

(4.2)

where
Q_{j}(a)
and
Q_{j}^{-1}(a)
are the diagonalizing matrices introduced in (
3.29
) and (
3.30
). From (
3.31
), one has

\mathbf{Q}^{-1}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{M}_{0}\mathbf{Q}=-{\rm i}\begin{pmatrix}\Omega(a,D)&0\\
0&-\Omega(a,D)\end{pmatrix}\triangleq-{\rm i}\boldsymbol{\Omega}(a,D),\qquad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*},\quad{\rm i}\Omega(a,D)\mathbf{e}_{j}\triangleq{\rm i}\Omega_{j}(a)\mathbf{e}_{j},

(4.3)

with
\Omega(a,D)
as in Lemma
3.1
(see also (
3.21
)) and
\Omega_{j}(a)
as in (
3.20
).
Let us consider the new unknown
\widetilde{r}\triangleq\mathbf{Q}^{-1}r.
Then the Hamiltonian system (
3.5
) becomes

\partial_{t}\widetilde{r}=\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathcal{J}\nabla H(\widetilde{r}),\qquad H(\widetilde{r})\triangleq\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{Q}\widetilde{r}).

(4.4)

We mention that the linearized equation at the equilibrium state
(\tilde{r}_{+},\tilde{r}_{-})=(0,0)
for the new Hamiltonian system is

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{L}_{0}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{J}\nabla H_{\mathbf{L}_{0}}(\rho_{+},\rho_{-}),

(4.5)

where

\mathbf{L}_{0}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}\triangleq\mathbf{Q}^{-1}\mathbf{M}_{0}\mathbf{Q}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{\Omega_{j}(a)}{{\rm i}j}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{j}^{+}\\
\rho_{j}^{-}\end{pmatrix}

and

H_{\mathbf{L}_{0}}(\rho_{+},\rho_{-})\triangleq\tfrac{1}{2}\left\langle\mathbf{L}_{0}\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}\,,\,\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\frac{\Omega_{j}(a)}{j}\Big(\big|\rho_{j}^{+}\big|^{2}+\big|\rho_{j}^{-}\big|^{2}\Big).

(4.6)

Note that the Hamiltonian
H_{\mathbf{L}_{0}}
is in normal form and, by Lemma
3.4
, the traveling quasi-periodic reversible solutions to (
4.5
) take the following form

\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{+}\\
\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix}(t,x)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{+}}A_{j}\cos\big(\Omega_{j}(a)-jx\big)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{-}}B_{j}\cos\big(\Omega_{j}(a)+jx\big)\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix},

with
\mathbb{S}_{+},\mathbb{S}_{-}
as in (
3.33
)-(
3.34
), for some coefficients
A_{j},B_{j}\in\mathbb{R}.

By (
4.1
), (
4.3
), (
4.5
), the new Hamiltonian system (
4.4
) associated with the Hamiltonian
H({\widetilde{r}})
writes

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{r}_{+}\\
\widetilde{r}_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{L}_{0}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{r}_{+}\\
\widetilde{r}_{-}\end{pmatrix}+\varepsilon X_{P_{H}}(\widetilde{r}),\qquad\mathcal{J}\mathbf{L}_{0}=\mathbf{Q}^{-1}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{M}_{0}\mathbf{Q}=-{\rm i}\boldsymbol{\Omega}(a,D),\qquad X_{P_{H}}(\widetilde{r})=\mathbf{Q}^{-1}X_{P_{\mathcal{E}}}(\mathbf{Q}\widetilde{r}),

(4.7)

where the Hamiltonian
H
reads

H=H_{\mathbf{L}_{0}}+\varepsilon P_{H},\qquad P_{H}({\widetilde{r}})\triangleq P_{{\mathcal{E}}}({\bf Q}{\widetilde{r}}).

(4.8)

Action-angle coordinates and embedded tori.

Next, we split the phase space into tangential and normal subspaces. We recall that the symmetrized tangential sets are introduced in (
3.36
). Then, we have the following decomposition of the space
\mathbf{L}_{0}^{2}(\mathbb{T})
in (
3.12
)

\mathbf{L}_{0}^{2}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}\overset{\perp}{\oplus}\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}

(4.9)

where
{\bf H}_{\overline{{\mathbb{S}}}}
is the finite dimensional
tangential subspace

\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}\triangleq\left\{v=\sum_{j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{+}}v_{j}^{+}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{j}+\sum_{j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-}}v_{j}^{-}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{j},\quad v_{j}^{\pm}\in\mathbb{C},\quad\overline{v_{-j}^{\pm}}=v_{j}^{\pm}\right\},

(4.10)

and
\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}
is the
normal subspace

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}\triangleq\left\{z=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{+}}z_{j}^{+}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{j}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-}}z_{j}^{-}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{j},\quad z_{j}^{\pm}\in\mathbb{C},\quad\overline{z_{-j}^{\pm}}=z_{j}^{\pm}\right\}.

(4.11)

The subspace
\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}
is the
\mathbf{L}_{0}^{2}
-orthogonal of the subspace
\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}
, and any
r\in\mathbf{L}_{0}^{2}
can be decomposed into
r=v+z
with
(v,z)\in\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}\times\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}.
The tangential and normal variables
v
and
z
are obtained by considering the orthogonal pojections
\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}
and
\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}
associated with the decomposition (
4.9
), explicitly given by

\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{+}&0\\
0&\Pi_{-}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}=\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{+}^{\perp}&0\\
0&\Pi_{-}^{\perp}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\Pi_{\pm}r\triangleq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{\pm}}r_{j}\mathbf{e}_{j}\qquad\Pi_{\pm}^{\perp}\triangleq\textnormal{Id}-\Pi_{\pm}.

On the tangential subspace
\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}
we introduce the action-angle variables
(I,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
as follows : fix some amplitudes
(\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{\pm})_{j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\pm}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\pm}}
with

\forall j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\pm},\quad\mathfrak{a}_{-j}^{\pm}=\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{\pm}.

Then, write the Fourier coefficients of any function
v\in\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}
in symplectic polar coordinates

\forall j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\pm},\quad v_{j}^{\pm}\triangleq\sqrt{(\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{\pm})^{2}+\tfrac{|j|}{2}I_{j}^{\pm}}\,e^{{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}^{\pm}},\qquad\textnormal{with}\qquad I_{-j}^{\pm}=I_{j}^{\pm},\qquad\vartheta_{-j}^{\pm}=-\vartheta_{j}^{\pm}.

The
actions
and
angles variables
are then given as the vectors

I\triangleq\Big(\big(I_{j}^{+}\big)_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{+}},\big(I_{j}^{-}\big)_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{-}}\Big)\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad\vartheta\triangleq\Big(\big(\vartheta_{j}^{+}\big)_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{+}},\big(\vartheta_{j}^{-}\big)_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{-}}\Big)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.

The above definitions allow to consider the following transformation

\mathbf{A}:\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}&\rightarrow&\mathbf{L}_{0}^{2}(\mathbb{T})\\
(\vartheta,I,z)&\mapsto&v(\vartheta,I)+z,\end{array}

(4.12)

where

v(\vartheta,I)\triangleq\sum_{j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{+}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{(\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{+})^{2}+\tfrac{|j|}{2}I_{j}^{+}}\,e^{{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}^{+}}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{j}+\sum_{j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\sqrt{(\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{-})^{2}+\tfrac{|j|}{2}I_{j}^{-}}\,e^{{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}^{-}}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{e}_{j}.

Straightforward calculations lead to

\forall\,j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{\pm},\quad\begin{cases}{\rm d}v_{j}^{\pm}=\frac{|j|e^{{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}^{\pm}}}{4\sqrt{(\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{\pm})^{2}+\frac{|j|}{2}I_{j}^{\pm}}}\,{\rm d}I_{j}^{\pm}+{\rm i}e^{{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}^{\pm}}\sqrt{(\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{\pm})^{2}+\tfrac{|j|}{2}I_{j}^{\pm}}\,{\rm d}\vartheta_{j}^{\pm},\vskip 5.69046pt\\
{\rm d}v_{-j}^{\pm}=\frac{|j|e^{-{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}^{\pm}}}{4\sqrt{(\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{\pm})^{2}+\frac{|j|}{2}I_{j}^{\pm}}}\,{\rm d}I_{j}^{\pm}-{\rm i}e^{-{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}^{\pm}}\sqrt{(\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{\pm})^{2}+\tfrac{|j|}{2}I_{j}^{\pm}}\,{\rm d}\vartheta_{j}^{\pm}.\end{cases}

Therefore,

\forall\,j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}},\quad{\rm d}v_{j}^{\pm}\wedge{\rm d}v_{-j}^{\pm}=\tfrac{{\rm i}|j|}{2}{\rm d}\vartheta_{j}^{\pm}\wedge{\rm d}I_{j}^{\pm}.

As a consequence, the symplectic 2-form in (
3.13
) reads

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{-}}\tfrac{1}{{\rm i}j}{\rm d}v_{j}^{-}\wedge{\rm d}v_{-j}^{-}-\sum_{j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{+}}\tfrac{1}{{\rm i}j}{\rm d}v_{j}^{+}\wedge{\rm d}v_{-j}^{+}+\mathscr{W}|_{\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{-}}{\rm d}\vartheta_{j}^{-}\wedge{\rm d}I_{j}^{-}-\sum_{j\in\mathbb{S}_{+}}{\rm d}\vartheta_{j}^{+}\wedge{\rm d}I_{j}^{+}+\mathscr{W}|_{\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}}.

The Poisson structure associated with
\mathscr{W}
is defined through

\{F,G\}\triangleq\mathscr{W}(X_{F},X_{G})=\langle\nabla F,\mathbf{J}\nabla G\rangle,\qquad\textnormal{where}\qquad\langle(\vartheta,I,z),(\vartheta^{\prime},I^{\prime},z^{\prime})\rangle\triangleq\vartheta\cdot\vartheta^{\prime}+I\cdot I^{\prime}+\langle z,z^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

(4.13)

and the unbounded transformation
\mathbf{J}
is given by

\mathbf{J}:(\vartheta,I,z)\mapsto\big(\mathtt{J}I,-\mathtt{J}\vartheta,\mathcal{J}z\big),\qquad\mathtt{J}\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}-\mathtt{I}_{d_{+}}&0\\
0&\mathtt{I}_{d_{-}}\end{pmatrix},

(4.14)

with
\mathtt{I}_{d_{\pm}}
denoting the identity matrix of size
d_{\pm}.
Any application

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{d}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}

(4.15)

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\big(\vartheta(\varphi),I(\varphi),z(\varphi,\cdot)\big)

is called an
embedded torus
and can be identified with a function
\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\mapsto r(\varphi,\cdot)\in\mathbf{L}_{0}^{2}
through the transformation
\mathbf{A}.
Let us emphasize that in this new unknowns, each trivial embedding
\varphi\mapsto(\varphi,0,0)
corresponds to one of the equilibrium solutions in Lemma
3.4
. In the new variables, according to (
4.8
) and (
4.6
), we obtain a new Hamiltonian system associated with

H_{\varepsilon}\triangleq H\circ\mathbf{A}=-\big(\mathtt{J}\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\big)\cdot I+\tfrac{1}{2}\big\langle\mathbf{L}_{0}z,z\big\rangle_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\varepsilon\mathcal{P},\qquad\mathcal{P}\triangleq P_{H}\circ\mathbf{A}.

(4.16)

This corresponds to the vector field

X_{H_{\varepsilon}}\triangleq\big(\mathtt{J}\partial_{I}H_{\varepsilon},-\mathtt{J}\partial_{\vartheta}H_{\varepsilon},\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}\nabla_{z}H_{\varepsilon}\big).

Actually, in the sequel, it is more convenient to replace the frequency vector by
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and to consider the following modified Hamiltonian

H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\triangleq\alpha\cdot I+\tfrac{1}{2}\big\langle\mathbf{L}_{0}z,z\big\rangle_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\varepsilon\mathcal{P},

(4.17)

and the nonlinear operator

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i,\alpha,a,\omega)\triangleq\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}i(\varphi)-X_{H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}}\big(i(\varphi)\big)=\begin{pmatrix}\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\vartheta(\varphi)-\alpha-\varepsilon\mathtt{J}\partial_{I}\mathcal{P}\big(i(\varphi)\big)\\
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}I(\varphi)+\varepsilon\mathtt{J}\partial_{\vartheta}\mathcal{P}\big(i(\varphi)\big)\\
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}z(\varphi)-\mathcal{J}\mathbf{L}_{0}z(\varphi)-\varepsilon\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}\mathcal{J}\nabla_{z}\mathcal{P}\big(i(\varphi)\big)\end{pmatrix}.

(4.18)

If
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i,\alpha)=0
, then the embedding
{\varphi}\mapsto i({\varphi})
is an invariant torus for the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}}
filled with quasi-periodic solutions with frequency
\omega
.
Observe that

\mathscr{F}_{0}\big(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}},-\mathtt{J}\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a),a,\omega_{\textnormal{Eq}}(a)\big)=0,\qquad i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}}(\varphi)\triangleq(\varphi,0,0),

(4.19)

which is just a reformulation of Lemma
3.4
. Our purpose now becomes to find invariant tori
i
near the trivial solution
i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}}
. Therefore, we define the following quantities

\Theta(\varphi)\triangleq\vartheta(\varphi)-\varphi\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\mathfrak{I}\triangleq(\Theta,I,z)=i-i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}}.

(4.20)

In Section
7
, we shall apply the Nash-Moser implicit function theorem in order to find a non-trivial zeros

\big(i(a,\omega),\alpha(a,\omega)\big)=\big(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}}+{\mathfrak{I}}(a,\omega),\alpha(a,\omega)\big)
of the functional
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.18
).

Reversible and traveling tori.

We are looking for
r
a reversible traveling wave, namely satisfying
r({\varphi},x)=r(-{\varphi},-x)
and
r(\varphi-\vec{\jmath}y,x)=r(\varphi,x+y)
(see (
A.4
)). In the language of embedded tori, we have the following definition.

Definition 4.1
.

We define

\mathfrak{S}:(\vartheta,I,z)\mapsto(-\vartheta,I,\mathscr{S}z),\qquad\mathfrak{T}_{y}:(\vartheta,I,z)\mapsto(\vartheta-\vec{\jmath}y,I,\mathscr{T}_{y}z),

(4.21)

with
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{T}_{y}
the involution and translations defined in (
3.14
).
A torus
i=(\vartheta,I,z):\mathbb{T}^{d}\to{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\bf H}_{\overline{S}_{0}}^{\perp}
is called:

•

reversible if, for any
{\varphi}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,
\mathfrak{S}i({\varphi})=i(-{\varphi})
;

•

anti-reversible if, for any
{\varphi}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,
-\mathfrak{S}i({\varphi})=i(-{\varphi})
;

•

traveling if, for any
{\varphi}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
and
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\mathfrak{T}_{y}(i)(\varphi)=i(\varphi-\vec{\jmath}y)
.

By (
4.17
), (
4.16
), (
4.4
), (
3.15
), (
3.16
) and Definition
4.1
, we note that the Hamiltonians
H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}
are invariant under the involution
\mathfrak{S}
and the translations
\mathfrak{T}_{y}
(for any
y\in\mathbb{T}
), namely

H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\circ\mathfrak{S}=H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}=H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\circ\mathfrak{T}_{y}.

As we look for
\vartheta({\varphi})
of the form
\vartheta({\varphi})={\varphi}+\Theta({\varphi})
with a
(2\pi)^{d}
-periodic function
\Theta:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
{\varphi}\mapsto\Theta({\varphi})
(see (
4.20
)), in order to justify the momentum preserving property of the constructed approximate inverse in Section
5
, we consider the
traveling variation operator

\forall\,y\in\mathbb{T},\quad\mathfrak{DT}_{y}\begin{pmatrix}\Theta\\
I\\
z\end{pmatrix}\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\Theta\\
I\\
\mathscr{T}_{y}z\end{pmatrix}.

Definition 4.2
.

We say that an element
{\varphi}\mapsto g({\varphi})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}
is a traveling wave variation if and only if

\forall\,y\in\mathbb{T},\quad\forall\,\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\quad\mathfrak{DT}_{y}g(\varphi)=g(\varphi-\vec{\jmath}y).

Recall the following result from
[
13
, Lem. 3.34]
.

Lemma 4.1
.

We have the following:

(i)

According to the Definition
A.3
, a linear operator
A(\varphi)
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}
is momentum preserving if and only if

\forall\,y\in\mathbb{T},\quad\forall\,\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\quad A(\varphi-\vec{\jmath}y)\circ\mathfrak{DT}_{y}=\mathfrak{DT}_{y}\circ A(\varphi).

(ii)

If
g
is a traveling wave variation and
A(\varphi)
is a momentum preserving operator, then
\varphi\mapsto A(\varphi)g(\varphi)
is a traveling wave variation.

5
Tame estimates and approximate right inverse

In this section, we provide the material to run the Nash-Moser analysis in Section
7
in order to solve (recall (
4.18
), (
4.17
))

\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i,\alpha,a,\omega)=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}i-X_{H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}}(i)=0.

(5.1)

Here start topological considerations and we refer the reader to the functional framework introduced in the Appendix
A
. We define
\mathcal{O}
the initial set of parameters – where
(a,\omega)
are taken – as follows

{\mathcal{O}}\triangleq(a_{0},a_{1})\times B_{R_{0}}(0),\qquad 0<a_{0}<a_{1},

(5.2)

where
B_{R_{0}}(0)
is the open ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
such that, for some
R_{0}>0
,

\omega_{\rm Eq}([a_{0},a_{1}])\subset B_{R_{0}/2}(0).

(5.3)

We also fix
(d,\gamma,q,s_{0},S)
as in (
A.1
). Let us insist on the fact that one needs a
C^{q}
-regularity with respect to the parameters
(a,\omega)
in order to apply the transversality analysis of Section
3.3
required later for estimating the measure of the final Cantor set in Section
7.2
. In what follows, we assume that

\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\|\mathfrak{I}\|_{s_{0}+\sigma_{\rm max}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant 1,

(5.4)

where
\sigma_{\rm max}>0
is a fixed loss of derivatives depending on
d,q
, and where

\|\mathfrak{I}\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\triangleq\|\Theta\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|I\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|z\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

(5.5)

is the norm associated with the periodic component
\mathfrak{I}
as in (
4.20
).

5.1
Tame estimates for the functional

To run the Nash-Moser scheme, it is essential to get tame estimates on the functional, its linearized operator and second order differential in a neighbourhood of the trivial solution and its approximate right inverse. Tame estimates are linear in high regularity norm with potential loss of derivatives. They are the key point to close the induction argument. Here we provide the tame estimates for these quantities except the approximate inverse, which needs a special analysis performed in the next sections.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
{\mathfrak{I}}
in (
4.20
) satisfy (
5.4
). Then, for any
s\geqslant s_{0}
and for all
{\widehat{i}}\triangleq({\widehat{\vartheta}},{\widehat{I}},{\widehat{w}})
, it holds

\displaystyle\|X_{\mathcal{P}}\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,q,d}1+\|\mathfrak{I}\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(5.6)

\displaystyle\|{\rm d}_{i}X_{\mathcal{P}}[\hat{i}]\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,q,d}\|\hat{i}\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\mathfrak{I}\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\hat{i}\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(5.7)

\displaystyle\|{\rm d}_{i}^{2}X_{\mathcal{P}}[\hat{i},\hat{i}]\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,q,d}\|\hat{i}\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\hat{i}\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\mathfrak{I}\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\left(\|\hat{i}\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right)^{2}.

(5.8)

Proof.

From the explicit expression of the vector field
X_{P_{\mathcal{E}}}
in (
4.1
), one readily obtains

{\rm d}_{r}X_{P_{\mathcal{E}}}(r)[\rho]=\begin{pmatrix}-\rho_{+}\partial_{x}r_{+}-r_{+}\partial_{x}\rho_{+}\\
-\rho_{-}\partial_{x}r_{-}-r_{-}\partial_{x}\rho_{-}\end{pmatrix},\qquad{\rm d}_{r}^{2}X_{P_{\mathcal{E}}}(r)[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]=\begin{pmatrix}-\rho_{+}^{1}\partial_{x}\rho_{+}^{2}-\rho_{+}^{2}\partial_{x}\rho_{+}^{1}\\
-\rho_{-}^{1}\partial_{x}\rho_{-}^{2}-\rho_{-}^{2}\partial_{x}\rho_{-}^{1}\end{pmatrix}.

As a consequence, by Lemma
A.1
-
(i)
, we have the following estimates for any
s\geqslant s_{0}

\displaystyle\|X_{P_{\mathcal{E}}}(r)\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,q,d}\|r\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|r\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(5.9)

\displaystyle\|{\rm d}_{r}X_{P_{\mathcal{E}}}(r)[\rho]\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,q,d}\|r\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(5.10)

\displaystyle\|{\rm d}_{r}^{2}X_{P_{\mathcal{E}}}(r)[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,q,d}\|\rho_{1}\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\rho_{1}\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.11)

Combining (
5.9
), (
5.10
) and (
5.11
) together with the continuity of
\mathbf{Q}^{\pm 1}
on
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T}),
we get for any
s\geqslant s_{0},

\displaystyle\|X_{P_{H}}(\widetilde{r})\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,q,d}\|\widetilde{r}\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\widetilde{r}\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(5.12)

\displaystyle\|{\rm d}_{\widetilde{r}}X_{P_{H}}(\widetilde{r})[\rho]\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,q,d}\|\widetilde{r}\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\widetilde{r}\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(5.13)

\displaystyle\|{\rm d}_{\widetilde{r}}^{2}X_{P_{H}}(\widetilde{r})[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,q,d}\|\rho_{1}\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\rho_{1}\|_{s+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{s_{0}+1}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.14)

Besides, observe that

\forall\,\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d},\quad\|\partial_{\vartheta}^{\alpha}\partial_{I}^{\beta}v(\vartheta,I)\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim 1+\|\mathfrak{I}\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

Then, in view of the definition of the transformation
\mathbf{A}
in (
4.12
) and the definition of
\mathcal{P}
in (
4.16
), we get from (
5.12
), (
5.13
) and (
5.14
) the desired estimates.
∎

5.2
Approximate right inverse

We shall construct an approximate right inverse of the corresponding linearized operator at any given reversible and quasi-periodic traveling state
(i_{0},\alpha_{0})
near the flat torus

{\rm d}_{(i,\alpha)}\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0},\alpha_{0})=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}i_{0}-{\rm d}_{i}X_{H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha_{0}}}(i_{0})-\Big(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathtt{J}\widehat{\alpha}\\
0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\Big).

(5.15)

To achieve this goal, we make appeal to the Berti-Bolle approach
[
12
]
where they approximately decouple the linearized equations through a triangular system in the action-angle-normal variables. Here, we shall implement a slightly simplified version of this strategy adapted to the vectorial case and developed in
[
58
, Section 6]
; we work directly with the original torus
i_{0}=(\vartheta_{0},I_{0},z_{0})
instead of introducing an intermediate isotropic one as in
[
12
]
. In addition, in our case, we shall also follow the traveling property preservation in the spirit of
[
13
,
14
]
in order to guarantee the construction of a traveling quasi-periodic solution at the end of the scheme.

Let us consider
G_{0}
the diffeomorphism
G_{0}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}
defined by

\begin{pmatrix}\vartheta\\
I\\
z\end{pmatrix}\triangleq G_{0}\begin{pmatrix}\phi\\
\mathtt{y}\\
w\end{pmatrix}\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\vartheta_{0}(\phi)\\
I_{0}(\phi)+\mathtt{L}_{1}(\phi)\mathtt{y}+\mathtt{L}_{2}(\phi)w\\
z_{0}(\phi)+w\end{pmatrix},

(5.16)

where

\mathtt{L}_{1}(\phi)\triangleq\mathtt{J}[\partial_{\varphi}\vartheta_{0}(\phi)]^{-\top},\qquad\mathtt{L}_{2}(\phi)\triangleq\mathtt{J}[(\partial_{\vartheta}\widetilde{z}_{0})(\vartheta_{0}(\phi))]^{\top}\mathcal{J}^{-1},\qquad\widetilde{z}_{0}(\vartheta)\triangleq z_{0}\big(\vartheta_{0}^{-1}(\vartheta)\big).

The tensors
\mathcal{J}
and
{\mathtt{J}}
have been introduced in (
3.5
) and (
4.14
), respectively. In the new coordinates, the torus
i_{0}
is trivial (see (
4.19
)), that is

G_{0}\big(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}}(\varphi)\big)=G_{0}(\varphi,0,0)=i_{0}(\varphi).

Straightforward calculations similar to
[
13
, Lem. 6.3]
show that the transformation
G_{0}
is invariant under the transformations
\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{y}
as in (
4.1
), namely

G_{0}\circ\mathfrak{S}=\mathfrak{S}\circ G_{0}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\forall\,y\in\mathbb{T},\quad G_{0}\circ\mathfrak{T}_{y}=\mathfrak{T}_{y}\circ G_{0}.

(5.17)

In what follows, we denote

\widetilde{G}_{0}(\phi,y,w,\alpha)\triangleq\big(G_{0}(\phi,y,w),\alpha\big)

(5.18)

the diffeomorphism acting as the identity on the
\alpha
-component. We also quantify how an embedded torus
i_{0}(\mathbb{T})
is approximately invariant for the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha_{0}}}
in terms of the “error function”

Z\triangleq(Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3})\triangleq\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0},\alpha_{0})=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}i_{0}-X_{H^{\alpha_{0}}_{\varepsilon}}(i_{0}).

(5.19)

We now conjugate the linear operator
{\rm d}_{(i,\alpha)}\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0},{\alpha}_{0})
in (
5.15
) with the linear change of variables given by

DG_{0}\big(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}}({\varphi})\big)\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\phi}\\
\widehat{y}\\
\widehat{w}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\varphi}\vartheta_{0}(\varphi)&0&0\\
\partial_{\varphi}I_{0}(\varphi)&\mathtt{L}_{1}(\varphi)&\mathtt{L}_{2}(\varphi)\\
\partial_{\varphi}z_{0}(\varphi)&0&\textnormal{Id}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\phi}\\
\widehat{y}\\
\widehat{w}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.20)

Although the transformation
G_{0}
introduced in (
5.16
) is not symplectic and the
nonlinear Hamiltonian structure is no longer preserved, the conjugation of the linearized operator
via the linear change of variables
DG_{0}(i_{\rm triv})
leads to a triangular system with small errors of size
Z
as in (
5.19
). Yet we emphasize that the Hamiltonian structure
of the original system is of paramount importance, namely in the construction of the transformation
G_{0}
leading to the final triangular system.
More precisely, we have the following result.

Proposition 5.1
.

The following properties hold true.

(i)

The map
DG_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})
is reversibility and momentum preserving in the sense of Lemma
4.1
.

(ii)

The following conjugation formula holds:

\displaystyle[DG_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})]^{-1}{\rm d}_{(i,\alpha)}\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0},\alpha_{0})D\widetilde{G}_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})[\widehat{\phi},\widehat{y},\widehat{w},\widehat{\alpha}]=\mathbb{D}[\widehat{\phi},\widehat{y},\widehat{w},\widehat{\alpha}]+\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\phi},\widehat{y},\widehat{w}],

(5.21)

where:

•

the operator
\mathbb{D}
has the triangular form

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}[\widehat{\phi},\widehat{y},\widehat{w},\widehat{\alpha}]\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\widehat{\phi}-\big[K_{20}(\varphi)\widehat{y}+K_{11}^{\top}(\varphi)\widehat{w}+\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\top}(\varphi)\widehat{\alpha}\big]\\
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\widehat{y}+\mathcal{B}(\varphi)\widehat{\alpha}\\
\mathscr{L}_{\omega}\widehat{w}-\mathcal{J}\big[K_{11}(\varphi)\widehat{y}+\mathtt{L}_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)\widehat{\alpha}\big]\end{pmatrix},

(5.22)

where
\mathcal{B}(\varphi)
and
K_{20}(\varphi)
are
d\times d
real matrices given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{L}_{1}^{-1}(\varphi)\partial_{\varphi}I_{0}(\varphi)\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\top}(\varphi)+[\partial_{\varphi}z_{0}(\varphi)]^{\top}\mathtt{L}_{2}^{\top}(\varphi),

(5.23)

\displaystyle K_{20}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\varepsilon\mathtt{L}_{1}^{\top}(\varphi)(\partial_{II}\mathcal{P})\big(i_{0}(\varphi)\big)\mathtt{L}_{1}(\varphi),

(5.24)

\mathscr{L}_{\omega}
is the linearized operator in the normal directions

\mathscr{L}_{\omega}\triangleq\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\bot}\big(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\mathcal{J}K_{02}(\varphi)\big)\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\bot}

(5.25)

with
K_{02}(\varphi)
the linear self-adjoint operator of
\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}
given by

\displaystyle K_{02}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\triangleq(\partial_{z}\nabla_{z}K_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha_{0}})\big(i_{0}(\varphi)\big)+\varepsilon\mathtt{L}_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)(\partial_{II}\mathcal{P})\big(i_{0}(\varphi)\big)\mathtt{L}_{2}(\varphi)

(5.26)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\mathtt{L}_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)(\partial_{zI}\mathcal{P})\big(i_{0}(\varphi)\big)+\varepsilon(\partial_{I}\nabla_{z}\mathcal{P})\big(i_{0}(\varphi)\big)\mathtt{L}_{2}(\varphi),

(5.27)

and where
K_{11}(\varphi)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp})
is given by

\displaystyle K_{11}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\varepsilon\mathtt{L}_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)(\partial_{II}\mathcal{P})\big(i_{0}(\varphi)\big)\mathtt{L}_{1}(\varphi)+\varepsilon(\partial_{I}\nabla_{z}\mathcal{P})\big(i_{0}(\varphi)\big)\mathtt{L}_{1}(\varphi);

•

the remainder
\mathbb{E}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\phi},\widehat{y},\widehat{w}]

\displaystyle\triangleq[DG_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})]^{-1}\partial_{\varphi}Z(\varphi)\widehat{\phi}+\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\mathcal{A}(\varphi)\big[K_{20}(\varphi)\widehat{y}+K_{11}^{\top}(\varphi)\widehat{w}\big]-R_{10}(\varphi)\widehat{y}-R_{01}(\varphi)\widehat{w}\\
0\end{pmatrix},\qquad

(5.28)

where
\mathcal{A}(\varphi)
and
R_{10}(\varphi)
are
d\times d
real matrices defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\triangleq[\partial_{\varphi}\vartheta_{0}(\varphi)]^{\top}{\mathtt{J}}\partial_{\varphi}I_{0}(\varphi)-[\partial_{\varphi}I_{0}(\varphi)]^{\top}{\mathtt{J}}\partial_{\varphi}\vartheta_{0}(\varphi)-[\partial_{\varphi}z_{0}(\varphi)]^{\top}\mathcal{J}^{-1}\partial_{\varphi}z_{0}(\varphi),

\displaystyle R_{10}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\triangleq[\partial_{\varphi}Z_{1}(\varphi)]^{\top}[\partial_{\varphi}\vartheta_{0}(\varphi)]^{-\top},

and where
R_{01}(\varphi)\in{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp},{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is defined by

\displaystyle R_{01}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\triangleq[\partial_{\varphi}Z_{1}(\varphi)]^{\top}[(\partial_{\vartheta}\widetilde{z}_{0})(\vartheta_{0}(\varphi))]^{\top}\mathcal{J}^{-1}-[\partial_{\varphi}Z_{3}(\varphi)]^{\top}\mathcal{J}^{-1}.

Proof.

The proof of item
(ii)
and the fact that
DG_{0}
is reversibility preserving is given in
[
58
, Prop. 6.1]
. The momentum preserving property for
DG_{0}
in item
(i)
follows by differentiating the second equation in (
5.17
) and Lemma
4.1
-
(i)
.
∎

Let us emphasize that the error term
\mathbb{E}
vanishes at an exact solution. This is clear from its definition in (
5.28
) and some algebraic manipulations on the matrix
\mathcal{A}(\varphi)
(see
[
58
, Lem. 6.2]
). Therefore, we are left to invert the triangular system associated with the operator
\mathbb{D}.
For this, we need to invert the operators
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\omega}.
We introduce the inverse operator
(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi})_{\rm ext}^{-1}
defined as follows (recalling the definition of the cuf-off function
\chi(\,\cdot\,)
in (
3.22
))

\forall\,h=\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}h_{\ell}{\bf e}_{\ell},\qquad(\omega\cdot\partial_{{\varphi}})_{\rm ext}^{-1}h\triangleq\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\chi((\omega\cdot\ell)\gamma^{-1}|\ell|^{\tau_{1}})}{{\rm i}(\omega\cdot\ell)}h_{\ell}{\bf e}_{\ell}.

(5.29)

It is an exact inverse of
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}
on the Diophantine set

\mathtt{DC}(\gamma,\tau_{1})\triangleq\bigcap_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\Big\{\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad|\omega\cdot\ell|>\frac{\gamma}{|\ell|^{\tau_{1}}}\Big\},\qquad\tau_{1}>d-1.

(5.30)

The construction of a right inverse to
\mathscr{L}_{\omega}
is the purpose of Section
6
, see Proposition
6.10
. We state here the corresponding result in the following assumption.

Assumption 1
.

(Full right inverse for
{\mathscr{L}}_{\omega}
):
Let
(\gamma,q,d,\tau_{1},\tau,s_{0},s_{h},\mu_{2})
as in (
A.1
), (
5.30
), (
6.92
) and (
6.178
). There exists
\sigma_{5}\triangleq\sigma_{5}(\tau_{1},\tau,q,d)>0
such that, if (
5.4
) holds with
s_{h}+\sigma_{5}\leqslant s_{0}+\sigma_{\rm max},
then there exists a linear operator
\widehat{\mathtt{T}}
defined in
\mathcal{O}
such that, for any traveling wave function
h
, we have that
{\widehat{{\mathtt{T}}}}h
is a traveling wave function satisfying the estimate

\forall\,s\in\,[s_{0},S],\quad\|\widehat{\mathtt{T}}\rho\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim_{s,q,d}\gamma^{-1}\big(\|\rho\|_{s+\sigma_{5}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{s+\sigma_{5}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{s_{0}+\sigma_{5}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big).

(5.31)

In addition, if
h
is anti-reversible, then
{\widehat{{\mathtt{T}}}}h
is reversible.
Moreover, in a Cantor set

\mathtt{G}\triangleq\mathtt{G}(\gamma,\tau_{1},\tau,i_{0})\subset(a_{0},a_{1})\times\mathtt{DC}(\gamma,\tau_{1}),
we have

{\mathscr{L}}_{\omega}{\widehat{{\mathtt{T}}}}=\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}.

The following proposition shows that the principal term
\mathbb{D}
in (
5.21
) of Proposition
5.1
-
(ii)
has an exact inverse. In the following, we denote

\|(\phi,y,w,\alpha)\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,{\mathcal{O}}}\triangleq\max\{\|(\phi,y,w)\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,{\mathcal{O}}},|\alpha|^{q,\gamma,{\mathcal{O}}}\}.

(5.32)

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
(\gamma,d,q,\tau_{1},\tau,s_{0})
as in (
A.1
), (
5.30
) and (
6.178
). There exists
\sigma_{6}\triangleq\sigma_{6}(\tau_{1},\tau,q,d)>0
such that if (
5.4
) holds with
s_{h}+\sigma_{6}\leqslant s_{0}+\sigma_{\rm max}
, then there exists an operator
[{\mathbb{D}}]_{\textnormal{ext}}^{-1}
, well-defined on the whole set of parameters
\mathcal{O},
such that, for any
g\triangleq(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
anti-reversible traveling wave variation, namely satisfying (see also Definition
4.1
and Definition
4.2
)

g_{1}(\varphi)=g_{1}(-\varphi),\quad g_{2}(\varphi)=-g_{2}(-\varphi),\quad g_{3}(\varphi)=-(\mathscr{S}g_{3})(\varphi),\qquad\forall\,y\in{\mathbb{R}},\quad(\mathscr{T}_{y}g_{3})(\varphi)=g_{3}(\varphi-\vec{\jmath}y),

(5.33)

the function

[{\mathbb{D}}]_{\textnormal{ext}}^{-1}g=({\widehat{\phi}},{\widehat{y}},{\widehat{w}},{\widehat{\alpha}})

satisfies the estimate

\forall\,s\in[s_{0},S],\quad\|[{\mathbb{D}}]_{\textnormal{ext}}^{-1}g\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim_{s,q,d}\gamma^{-1}\big(\|g\|_{s+\sigma_{6}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|{\mathfrak{I}}_{0}\|_{s+\sigma_{6}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|g\|_{s_{0}+\sigma_{6}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big)

(5.34)

and for all
(a,\omega)\in\mathtt{G}
one has

{\mathbb{D}}[{\mathbb{D}}]_{\textnormal{ext}}^{-1}=\textnormal{Id}.

(5.35)

Moreover, the first three components
({\widehat{\phi}},{\widehat{y}},{\widehat{w}})
of
[{\mathbb{D}}]_{\textnormal{ext}}^{-1}g
form a reversible traveling wave variation, according to Definition
4.1
.

Proof.

The proof of the existence of
\big([{\mathbb{D}}]_{\textnormal{ext}}^{-1}\big)
and of (
5.34
), (
5.35
) is given in
[
57
, Prop. 6.3]
. We are left to prove the claim that
({\widehat{\phi}},{\widehat{y}},{\widehat{w}})
is a reversible traveling wave variation. We have that
[{\mathbb{D}}]_{\textnormal{ext}}^{-1}g
is the solution of the linear system, for any
(a,\omega)\in{\mathtt{G}}
,

\mathbb{D}[{\widehat{\phi}},{\widehat{y}},{\widehat{w}},{\widehat{\alpha}}]=g

(5.36)

with
\mathbb{D}
as in (
5.22
) and
g=(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
satisfying (
5.33
). This is a triangular system. By Assumption
1
, the operator
{\mathscr{L}}_{\omega}
has a right inverse that is reversible and momentum preserving. Therefore, one solves the third equation in (
5.36
). As for the first and the second equations, we point out that the unknown
{\widehat{\alpha}}
is indeed used here to remove the average in
{\varphi}
from the first equation in (
5.36
). Consequently, these equations can be solved by using the operator
[\omega\cdot\partial_{{\varphi}}]_{\rm ext}^{-1}
in (
5.29
), which preserves the reversible structure.
Then, following the proof of
[
57
, Prop. 6.3]
and arguing as in
[
13
, Prop. 6.5]
, one concludes that
({\widehat{\phi}},{\widehat{y}},{\widehat{w}})
is indeed a reversible traveling wave variation.
∎

In view of (
5.21
) in Proposition
5.1
-
(ii)
, we obtain the following decomposition

\displaystyle{\rm d}_{(i,\alpha)}\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0},\alpha_{0})

\displaystyle=DG_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})\,{\mathbb{D}}\,[D\widetilde{G}_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})]^{-1}+DG_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})\,{\mathbb{E}}\,[D\widetilde{G}_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})]^{-1}.

Applying the operator

{\rm T}_{0}\triangleq{\rm T}_{0}(i_{0})\triangleq D{\widetilde{G}}_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})\,[{\mathbb{D}}]_{\textnormal{ext}}^{-1}\,[DG_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})]^{-1}

(5.37)

to the right of the last identity, for all
(a,\omega)\in\mathtt{G},

\displaystyle{\rm d}_{(i,\alpha)}\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0},\alpha_{0}){\rm T}_{0}-\textnormal{Id}=\mathfrak{E},\qquad\mathfrak{E}\triangleq DG_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})\,{\mathbb{E}}\,[D\widetilde{G}_{0}(i_{\textnormal{\tiny{triv}}})]^{-1}{\rm T}_{0}.

Consequently, the operator
{\rm T}_{0}
is an approximate right inverse for
{\rm d}_{(i,\alpha)}\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0},\alpha_{0})
. More precisely, we have the following result.

Theorem 5.1
.

(Approximate inverse)

Let
(\gamma,q,d,\tau_{1},\tau,s_{0},\mu_{2},s_{h},S)
satisfy (
A.1
), (
5.30
), (
6.92
) and (
6.178
). There exists
{\overline{\sigma}}={\overline{\sigma}}(\tau_{1},\tau,d,q)>0
such that, if (
5.4
) holds with
s_{h}+\overline{\sigma}\leqslant s_{0}+\sigma_{\rm max}
,
then, for any smooth
g=(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
anti-reversible traveling wave variation, that is satisfying (
5.33
),
the operator
{\rm T}_{0}
defined in (
5.37
) satisfies

\forall\,s\in[s_{0},S],\quad\|{\rm T}_{0}g\|_{s}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim_{s,q,d}\gamma^{-1}\big(\|g\|_{s+{\overline{\sigma}}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{s+{\overline{\sigma}}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|g\|_{s_{0}+\overline{\sigma}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big).

(5.38)

Moreover, the first three components of
{\rm T}_{0}g
form a reversible traveling wave variation, according to Definition
4.1
and Definition
4.2
.
Finally,
{\rm T}_{0}
is an approximate right inverse of
{\rm d}_{(i,\alpha)}\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0},\alpha_{0})
on the Cantor set
\mathtt{G}
, that is for all
(a,\omega)\in\mathtt{G}
one has

{\rm d}_{(i,\alpha)}\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0},\alpha_{0}){\rm T}_{0}-{\rm Id}=\mathfrak{E},

(5.39)

where the operator
\mathfrak{E}
is defined in the whole set
\mathcal{O}
and enjoy the following estimates

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{E}\rho\|_{s_{0}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{q,d}\gamma^{-1}\|\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0},\alpha_{0})\|_{s_{0}+\overline{\sigma}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{s_{0}+\overline{\sigma}}^{q,\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.40)

Proof.

The proof of (
5.38
), (
5.39
) and (
5.40
) follows as in
[
60
, Theorem 5.1]
and we omit it. The claim that the first three components of
{\rm T}_{0}g
form a reversible traveling wave variation follows from the definition of
{\rm T}_{0}
in (
5.37
), (
5.18
), Proposition
5.1
-
(i)
and Proposition
5.2
.
∎

6
Reducibility and inversion of the linearized operator

For this section, we consider again the parameters
(d,\gamma,q,s_{0},S)
fixed as in (
A.1
). Let
i_{0}=(\vartheta_{0},I_{0},z_{0})
be a fixed traveling reversible torus, i.e. satisfying (
4.1
), and with the additional smallness ansatz (
5.4
). Our aim here is to study the linearized operator in the normal directions taking the form (recall (
5.25
))

\mathscr{L}_{\omega}=\mathscr{L}_{\omega}(i_{0})=\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}\big(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\mathcal{J}K_{02}(\varphi)\big)\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}.

(6.1)

The ultimate goal is to prove Proposition
6.10
in order to obtain the existence of a full right inverse with tame estimates of the operator in (
6.1
) as previously stated in Assumption
1
.
We shall achieve this by a reducibility procedure consisting of a series of reversibility and momentum preserving, invertible transformations that conjugate
\mathscr{L}_{\omega}
to a reversible Fourier multiplier. We refer to Definition
A.3
for the definitions of real, reversible, reversibility and momentum preserving linear operators. We emphasize that the particular structure of our equations allows to run this reducibility procedure in the class of operator with homogeneous expansions (see Definition
A.7
), fundamental for applying the Egorov argument of Proposition
A.5
. For a concise presentation of the main reducibility steps, we refer the reader to the introductive Section
1.3
.

Notation:
Given two different states
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
satisfying (
5.4
) and a function
f:\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{H}_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}_{0}}^{\perp}\to X
(with
X
a set), we denote

\Delta_{12}f\triangleq f(i_{2})-f(i_{1}).

The periodic components are also denoted
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{k}(\varphi)\triangleq i_{k}(\varphi)-(\varphi,0,0),

k\in\{1,2\}.

6.1
Structure of the linearized operator in the normal directions

In this section we link the linear operator
\mathscr{L}_{\omega}
in (
6.1
), which acts only on the normal directions, to the one arising from the linearization at any approximate solution of the complete Hamiltonian system associated with
H
(see (
4.4
)). Furthermore, the latter operator will be suitably expanded in Proposition
6.2
for the purposes of the reducibility scheme in the forthcoming sections.

The proof of the following proposition can be adapted from
[
60
, Prop. 6.1]
. Let us mention that the traveling property can also be easily tracked (for a similar argument, see for instance
[
13
, Lemma 7.1]
).

Proposition 6.1
.

The following holds:

(i)

The torus
i_{0}
is associated with a state
